PREFACE

This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India has been prepared
for submission to the Governor under Article 151 of the Constitution of India.

The Report presents the results of the audit of the Departments of the
Government of Meghalaya under the Social, Economic and General Services.
However, audit observations on revenue receipts of the Government of
Meghalaya are excluded and covered in the Report on Revenue Services.

The cases mentioned in this Report are those, which came to notice in the course
of test audit of accounts for the year 2012-13 as well as those, which came to
notice in earlier years, but could not be dealt with in previous Audit Reports.
Matters relating to the period subsequent to 2012-13 have also been included,

wherever necessary.

The audits have been conducted in conformity with the Auditing Standards issued
by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India.


OVERVIEW


OVERVIEW

This Report contains chapters on Social, Economic and General Sectors and State
Public Sector Undertakings comprising three performance reviews and 12 audit
paragraphs, based on the audit of certain selected programmes and activities and the
financial transactions of the Government and Public Sector Undertakings.

Copies of the audit paragraphs and performance reviews were sent to the concerned
Secretaries to the State Government by the Accountant General (Audit) with a request
to furnish replies within six weeks. In respect of two reviews and seven audit
paragraphs in this Report, no response was received from the concerned Secretaries to
the State Government.

A synopsis of the important findings contained in this Report is presented in this
overview.

SOCIAL SECTOR

Indira Awaas Yojana

Indira Awaas Yojana (IAY) was launched in the State in 1996 to provide financial
assistance for construction of houses to rural BPL households. Implementation of IAY
in the State was adversely affected due to improper planning, failure of the
department to utilise available funds due to poor financial management and
ineffective implementation and monitoring of the scheme.

e Priority list and seniority order were not maintained while extending IAY benefit.

e Financial assistance of ¥ 1.81 crore was extended to 396 ineligible persons, who
were neither in the BPL list nor in the IAY Waitlist.

e 17 villages having BPL households were totally deprived of the IAY benefits.

e Deduction of Central assistance of = 5.27 crore and % 4.69 crore due to excess
carryover beyond permissible limit and short release of State share respectively.
There were cases of undue delay in release of funds by the State government.

e Convergence of IAY with other schemes like Total Sanitation Campaign, Rajiv
Gandhi Grameen Vidyutikaran Yojana, National Rural Water Supply
Programme, efc. was inadequate and ineffective in the State thereby failing to
achieve the objective of making IAY house a complete dwelling unit.

(Paragraph 1.2)



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

The Health & Family Welfare Department failed to make a Drug Testing Laboratory
functional even after eight years thereby rendering an expenditure of % 80.39 lakh
unfruitful.

(Paragraph 1.3)
The Health and Family Welfare Department extended undue patronage to an
unlicensed pharmaceutical firm which was functioning without compliance of ‘Good

Manufacturing Practices’ and extended undue financial benefit of 2.50 crore on
purchase of drugs from this firm for use in Government hospitals and health centres.

(Paragraph 1.5)

GENERAL SECTOR

Arbitrary action of the Public Works Department in execution of works for Nongstoin
and Nongpoh jails contrary to the State’s Financial Rules and exorbitant delay at
every stage resulted in an unfruitful expenditure of € 8.40 crore.

ECONOMIC SECTOR

Roads & Bridges funded by Non Lapsable Central Pool of Resources and North
Eastern Council

One of the objectives of the Non Lapsable Central Pool of Resources (NLCPR) and
the North Eastern Council (NEC) was to develop infrastructure, especially
construction of Roads and Bridges (R&B). Though the assets created by the NLCPR
and the NEC funds have helped the State to bridge the infrastructural gap to some
extent, certain aspects like poor planning, ineffective financial management, tardy
implementation and lack of proper monitoring and supervision adversely affected the
implementation of these projects.

e The State Planning Department neither submitted the priority lists in time nor
was proper analysis of infrastructural gaps conducted.

e Huge amount of funds lying unutilised with State Finance Department indicates
that the executing department, viz. Public Works Department (PWD), did not
have effective arrangement to absorb the allotted funds under NLCPR and NEC
funded R&B projects.

e PWD incurred an extra expenditure amounting to हैं 2.08 crore towards
inadmissible items. Certain items of work were executed in excess of the

e Allocation of same works to more than one contractor led to an avoidable
expenditure to the tune of हैं 1.59 crore. Injudicious decision of the PWD in
selecting the stone quarry led to an avoidable expenditure of हैं 97.90 lakh on
haulage.

e Funds amounting to हैं 1.38 crore were diverted from NLCPR and NEC funded

(Paragraph 3.2)

Tourism Development Activities in Meghalaya

Meghalaya is blessed with picturesque landscape of rolling hills, meandering rivers,
cascading waterfalls, lush forests, diverse flora and fauna and unique culture and
tradition. All these factors present a great potential for development of tourism in
Meghalaya. Tourism Department is responsible for the development of tourism sector
in the State.

e The Tourism Department did not effectively implement the Tourism Policy
2001. The Department had also not prepared Master Plan for integrated tourism
development, training needs analysis and formulation of marketing strategy/plan
as envisaged in the Tourism Policy of 2011.

e There were huge unspent balances of Central Financial Assistance ranging
between हैं 29.66 crore and हैं 63.22 crore during 2008-09 to 2012-13.

e There were undue delays ranging from 30 to 75 months in completion of
centrally sponsored projects which led to the State being denied the opportunity
to obtain new sanctions for 12 projects amounting to हैं 67.82 crore.

e In respect of State sponsored projects, no time schedule for completion of the
projects was set by the State Government. However, in all these projects, the
contractors failed to complete the work within the time specified as per the work
orders.

e Due to delay in release of NEC funds by the State Government, there was
avoidable extra cost of = 1.28 crore in respect of the project for improvement of
Marngar Lake in Ri-Bhoi District into a tourist spot.

e The capacity building programmes undertaken by the Department were not fully
effective.



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

e The Department had not established adequate internal control mechanisms for
effective functioning of the various offices under its control.

(Paragraph 3.3)

The objective of creating residential and non-residential infrastructure for seven newly
created Community & Rural Development Blocks was yet to be achieved due to the
failure of the Department to properly monitor the execution of these works as a result
of which an expenditure of 9.88 crore incurred so far was unfruitful.

(Paragraph 3.4)

Arbitrary decision of the Departmental Tender Committee of the Water Resources
Department in rejection of tender of a contractor in connection with the Ajagar Flow
Irrigation Project resulted in extra expenditure of हैं 51.57 lakh, besides delay in
completion of the project.

(Paragraph 3.6)

ECONOMIC SECTOR
(PUBLIC SECTOR UNDERTAKINGS)

Overview of State Public Sector Undertakings

Audit of Government companies is governed by Section 619 of the Companies Act,
1956. The accounts of Government companies are audited by Statutory Auditors
appointed by Comptroller and Auditor General of India (CAG).These accounts are
also subject to supplementary audit conducted by CAG. Audit of Statutory
corporations is governed by their respective legislations. As on 31 March 2013, the
State of Meghalaya had 14 working State Public Sector Undertakings (SPSUs) (12
companies and two statutory corporations) and one non-working company, which
employed 4518 employees. The working SPSUs registered a turnover of 461 crore
for 2012-13 as per their latest finalised accounts as of September 2013. This turnover
was equal to 2.48 per cent of State Gross Domestic Product indicating a moderate role
played by SPSUs in the economy. However, the working SPSUs incurred an overall
loss of % 82.08 crore in the aggregate for 2012-13 as per their latest finalised accounts
as on 30 September 2013.

Investments in SPSUs

As on 31 March 2013, of the total investment in SPSUs, 99.70 per cent was in
working SPSUs and the remaining 0.30 per cent in one non-working SPSU. This total
investment consisted of 33.53 per cent towards capital and 66.47 per cent in long-term
loans. The investment has increased by 14.83 per cent from % 1372.41 crore in
2007-08 to हैं 1575.92 crore in 2012-13.



Overview

Performance of SPSUs

During the year 2012-13, out of 14 working SPSUs, two SPSUs namely Meghalaya
Government Construction Corporation Limited and Meghalaya Industrial
Development Corporation Limited earned profit aggregating to ~ 2.44 crore and
remaining twelve SPSUs incurred loss of हैं 84.52 crore. The major losses were
incurred by Mawmluh Cherra Cements Limited (¥ 18.71 crore) and Meghalaya
Energy Corporation Limited (® 58.43 crore). The losses of working SPSUs were
mainly attributable to deficiencies in financial management, planning, implementation
of projects, operations and monitoring. A review of the latest Audit Reports of CAG
shows that the SPSUs incurred losses to the tune of % 60.23 crore which was
controllable with better management. Thus, there is tremendous scope to improve the
functioning of SPSUs and minimise losses.

Quality of accounts

The quality of accounts of SPSUs needs improvement. All the 17 accounts finalised by
working SPSUs during October 2012 to September 2013 received qualified
certificates. There were thirty seven instances of non-compliance with the Accounting
Standards. Reports of Statutory Auditors on internal control of the companies indicated
certain weak areas.

Arrears in accounts

Fourteen working SPSUs had arrears of 51 accounts as of September 2013. The
SPSUs need to set targets for the work relating to preparation of accounts with special
focus on arrears. There was one non-working company as on 31 March 2013.The
company is under liquidation process as all its employees had opted for VRS in
December 2006. As no purpose was served by keeping this non-working company in

existence, the Government needs to expedite the process of closure of this company.

(Paragraph 4.1)

Meghalaya Energy Corporation Limited

to a firm resulted in an extra irregular expenditure of हैं 226.57 lakh (Transformers:

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

1.1.1 Planning and conduct of Audit

Audit process starts with the assessment of risks faced by various departments of
Government based on expenditure incurred, criticality/complexity of activities, level
of delegated financial powers, assessment of overall internal controls and concerns.
The audits were conducted during 2012-13 involving expenditure of ¥ 1139.49 crore
(including expenditure pertaining to previous years audited during the year) of the
State Government under Social Sector. The chapter contains performance audit on
“Indira Awaas Yojana’ and three Compliance Audit paragraphs.

After completion of audit of each unit, Inspection Reports containing audit findings
are issued to the heads of the departments. The departments are requested to furnish
replies to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the Inspection Reports.
Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either settled or further action for
compliance is advised. The important audit observations arising out of these
Inspection Reports are processed for inclusion in the Audit Reports, which are
submitted to the Governor of State under Article 151 of the Constitution of India.

The major observations under Social Sector detected in audit during the year 2012-13
are given below.



Chapter I - Social Sector

COMMUNITY AND RURAL DEVELOPM

T DEPARTM

1.2. Indira Awaas Yojana

Highlights

Indira Awaas Yojana (IAY) was launched in the State in 1996 to provide financial
assistance for construction of houses to rural BPL households. Implementation of
FAY in the State was adversely affected due to improper planning, failure of the
department to utilise available funds, poor financial management and ineffective
implementation and monitoring of the scheme.

Priority list and seniority order were not maintained while extending IAY
benefit.

(Paragraph 1.2.7.1)

Financial assistance of हैं 1.81 crore was extended to 396 ineligible persons, who
were neither in the BPL list nor in the IAY Waitlist.
(Paragraph 1.2.7.2)

Deduction of Central assistance of € 5.27 crore and % 4.69 crore due to excess
carryover of funds beyond permissible limit and short release of State share
respectively.

(Paragraph 1.2.8.2)

(Paragraph 1.2.8.3)

Rajiv Gandhi Grameen Vidyutikaran Yojana, National Rural Water Supply

(Paragraph 1.2.10)

Involvement of officials at all levels for monitoring the implementation of the
JAY scheme in the State could not be ascertained in the absence of schedules of

(Paragraph 1.2.11.1)

1.2.1 Introduction

Indira Awaas Yojana (IAY), the flagship scheme of the Ministry of Rural
Development (MoRD), Government of India (Gol) for fulfilment of housing needs of
the rural poor population, was launched in May 1985 as a sub-scheme of Jawahar
Rozgar Yojana. It is being implemented as an independent scheme since 1“ January
1996. The IAY aims at helping rural people below the poverty-line (BPL) belonging
to SCs/STs, freed bonded labourers and non-SC/ST categories in construction of



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

dwelling units and upgradation of existing unserviceable kutcha houses by providing
assistance in the form of full grant. From 1995-96, the IAY benefits have been
extended to widows or next-of-kin of defence personnel killed in action. Benefits
have also been extended to ex-servicemen and retired members of the paramilitary
forces as long as they fulfil the normal eligibility conditions of IAY. Three per cent of
funds are reserved for the disabled BPL persons in rural areas. Since 2006-07, IAY
funds are also being earmarked for minorities.

IAY was implemented by the Community and Rural Development (C&RD)
Department in the State. IAY was funded on cost sharing basis between the Union
Government and the State Government in the ratio of 75:25 up to 2008-09. However,
from 2009-10 onwards, the funding was shared between Gol and State in the ratio of
90:10 respectively in the case of North-eastern states. Central assistance in the form of
grant-in-aid is directly released to the District Rural Development Agencies
(DRDAs), who in turn release the assistance to the Block Development Officers
(BDOs) at the Block level.

In Meghalaya, the financial assistance provided under IAY for new construction and
up-gradation up to March 2010 was हैं 38,500 and हैं 15,000 respectively and from
April 2010 onwards हैं 48,500 and & 15,000 respectively.

1.2.2 Organisational set-up

In Meghalaya, the C&RD Department is the nodal department for implementation of
IAY. The Project Directors of DRDAs under the C&RD Department are in charge at
the district level. The BDOs at the Block level were the implementing officers. The
organisational set up is given below:

Chart 1: Organisational Chart

Pr. Secretary
C&RD Department

Director, C&RD
Nodal Officer for IAY

Block Development Officer

1.2.4 Audit Objective

The objectives of this Performance audit were to assess whether:

>

a

Vv

the systems and procedures in place for identification and selection of the
target groups and the processes for allotment, construction and up-gradation of
dwelling units were adequate and conformed to the scheme provisions;

the allocation and release of funds under IAY were made in an adequate and
timely manner and that these were utilised economically and efficiently in
accordance with the scheme provisions;

the physical performance under IAY in terms of number of units constructe:
and upgraded as against planned and targeted and that the construction
corresponded to the quality and financial parameters set out in the scheme
guidelines;

the convergence of the IAY activities with other programmes as envisaged
was effectively achieved and ensured availability of a complete functiona
dwelling unit and;

the mechanism in place for monitoring and evaluation of the outcomes of the
programme were adequate and effective.

The findings were benchmarked against the criteria contained in the following
sources:

Vv

BPL Survey Report 2002.

1.2.6 Audit Methodology

The Performance audit commenced with an Entry Conference held on 15 May 2013
where the audit objectives, criteria, scope and methodology were discussed and
agreed upon.

Audit test checked the records of the C&RD Department, four DRDAs and eight
BDOs. The audit evidence was collected through issue of questionnaire, examination
of records, analysis of the data collected, discussion with the concerned authorities at
various levels and joint physical verification. Audit findings were discussed with the
officers of the C&RD Department led by the Principal Secretary at an Exit
Conference held on 28 November 2013 and their views incorporated at appropriate
places.

Audit Findings

1.2.7 Planning

Objective-1:_ Whether the systems and procedures in place for identification and|
selection of the target groups and the processes for allotment,
construction and up-gradation of dwelling units were adequate and|
conformed to the scheme provisions.

As per Para 2.1 and 2.2 of the IAY guidelines, the beneficiaries were to be selected
from the permanent IAY Waitlists prepared on the basis of BPL lists in order of
seniority. A separate list of SC/ST families in the order of their ranks may be derived
from the larger IAY list. Beneficiaries were to be prioritised on the basis of
categories, viz. SC/ST households, non-SC/ST BPL households, physically and
mentally challenged persons, etc.

The Gram Panchayats were to draw out the shelterless families from the BPL list
strictly according to the seniority list. In the absence of Panchayati Raaj Institution
(PRI) in Meghalaya, Village Councils were responsible for selection of beneficiaries.

Irregularities noticed in identification of the beneficiaries are discussed in the
subsequent paragraphs.

6


Chapter I - Social Sector

1.2.7.1 Maintenance of priority list and seniority order

Permanent IAY Waitlist in the State was prepared as per the BPL list of 2002.
However, the seniority as per BPL list was not maintained while preparing the
Permanent IAY Waitlist.

Audit observed that eight blocks in four selected districts did not have any category
wise priority list and beneficiaries were identified randomly which is in contravention
to the IAY guidelines. Moreover, it was also noticed that eligible BPL households
having low BPL score were not kept in the same order in the IAY Waitlist thereby
depriving them of the IAY benefit in the seniority order they deserved.

1.2.7.2 Assistance given to persons not appearing in the BPL List as well as the
TAY Waitlist

Out of 144 villages selected for test-check (2008-09 to 2012-13), it was noticed that in

90 villages, 396 ineligible rural households not appearing in the IAY Waitlist and

BPL List, were extended IAY benefit to the tune of = 1.81 crore. This action of the

epartment had not only deprived the genuine BPL households of housing facility,
ut also tantamount to misutilisation of Government funds to that extent. Details of
which are shown below:

practices, if any found.

1.2.8.2 Denial of benefit of Central assistance

As per Para 4.2 of IAY guidelines, Central assistance under the IAY was to be
released in two instalments and the second instalment was to be released on fulfilment
of some conditions, such as, (i) 60 per cent of the total available funds were utilised
while submitting the proposal for the second instalment, (ii) opening balance of the
District should not exceed 10 per cent of the funds available during the previous year
and (iii) the State Government released all its contribution due up to the date of the
application. In case, the opening balance exceeds the limit of 10 per cent, the Central
share of the excess was to be deducted proportionately. Similarly, in the event of
shortfall in State share, corresponding amount of Central share (i.e three times the
shortfall of State share) was to be deducted from the amount of Central share of the
second instalment.

It was noticed that during the period (2008-09 to 2012-13), Gol while releasing
second instalment of Central assistance, deducted हैं 5.27 crore on the ground of
excess carryover of fund beyond permissible limit as detailed below:

Table 1.2.7

(₹ in lakh)
Deductions on account of Excess
Year Districts Carryover
2008-09 East Khasi Hills 42.53
2009-10 East Khasi Hills 228.98
2010-11 West Garo Hills 202.14
2012-13 West Khasi Hills 53.15
Grand Total 526.80

Source: Sanction orders of MoRD

While admitting the fact, the Deputy Director, C&RD stated (August 2013) that this
was due to release of Gol’s second instalment at the very end of every year leaving no
scope for utilisation within the year. The reply of Deputy Director, C&RD is
irrelevant as the amount pointed out by audit was deducted at the time of release of
second instalment, which implies that the department was not able to fulfill the
conditions stipulated for release of second instalment.

Further scrutiny of sanction orders (2008-09 to 2012-13) revealed that while releasing
the second instalment, Central assistance of = 4.69 crore was deducted by Gol due to
short release of State share as detailed below:

directly into the beneficiaries’ account in a bank or post office. For this purpose, as
soon as the beneficiaries are selected, they should be asked to open a Bank/Post
Office account, in case they do not already have an account in any Bank or Post
Office, and to intimate the account number to the Gram Panchayat/BDO/DRDA, as
the case may be.

Audit, however, observed that funds of ¥ 5.95 crore and = 13.62 crore were paid to
the beneficiaries in cash and in bearer cheques respectively. Details are as shown

were no more eligible BPL minority households in the State had not been sent to the
MoRD.

Chapter I - Social Sector

1.2.9.2 Inventory register for IAY houses

Para 5.9 of the IAY guidelines provides that the implementing agencies should have a
complete inventory of houses constructed/upgraded under IAY, giving details of the
dates of start and the date of completion of construction of dwelling unit, name of the
village and block in which the house is located; occupation and category of
eneficiaries and other relevant particulars. Para 5.10 of the guidelines further
rovides that completion of dwelling unit in no case should take more than two years.

Audit, however, observed that except Mawkynrew block (East Khasi Hills District),
the remaining seven selected blocks had not maintained any inventory register for
IAY houses. Due to non-maintenance of detailed inventory of IAY houses, seven
locks in four selected districts were not able to ascertain the total number of houses
sanctioned to a AEC/village, number of houses completed, number of houses left
incomplete, funds released and utilised efc. for a given period.

Further, scrutiny of inventory register of Mawkynrew block revealed that even after

extending financial assistance of % 24.64 lakh, 83 IAY houses still remaine
incomplete (August 2013). Details are shown below:
Table 1.2.15
@ in lakh)
Year Total No. of Incomplete houses Financial assistance
beneficiaries provided
2008-09 93 4 1.07
2009-10 100 13 3.12
2010-11 224 66 20.45
Total 417 83 24.64

‘Source: Information furnished by BDO Mawkynrew, East Khasi Hills District
The BDOs of seven blocks while accepting the Audit observation assured to maintain
inventory of IAY houses with immediate effect. As regards Mawkynrew Block, while
admitting the fact, the concerned BDO stated (August 2013) that in some cases the
houses could not be completed due to illness or death of beneficiaries, and that the

matter would be investigated and report thereof would be furnished.

1.2.9.3. Location of IAY houses and availability of other amenities

Para 3.5 of the IAY guidelines provides that the IAY dwelling units should normally be
built on individual plots in the main habitation of the village. The houses can also be built
in a cluster within a habitation so as to facilitate the development of infrastructure such as
internal roads, drainage, drinking water supply, household and other common facilities.
Care should always be taken to see that the houses under the IAY are located close to the
village and not far away, so as to ensure safety and security, nearness to work place and
social communication. To the extent possible, the site should not be located in disaster
prone areas, for example frequently floodable areas.

Audit, however, observed that the IAY houses were made on the individual plots of land
and not in a cluster. As such, other infrastructural facilities such as internal roads,
drainage, drinking water supply and household toilets were not available to most of the



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

houses as verified during the physical verification of the beneficiaries. As such, such IAY
houses could not be termed as complete dwelling units as envisaged in the Scheme.

The Deputy Director, C&RD stated (August 2013) that cluster approach was adopted in
the State during implementation of IAY. However, construction of houses was not done
in group or at a single site as the land for construction of houses was allotted by the
community. Further, it was stated that geographical condition with no proper and
common road system in rural areas of the State, division of villages in groups might
deprive the deserving villages which are in urgent need of assistance.

1.2.9.4 Cost effectiveness and quality of materials

The IAY guidelines provide that effort should be made to utilise to the maximum
possible extent, local materials and cost effective disaster resistant and environment
friendly technologies developed by various institutions. Zilla Parishad/DRDA should
contact various organisations/institutions for seeking expertise information on
innovative technologies, materials, designs and methods to help beneficiaries in the
construction/upgradation of durable, cost effective houses and disaster resistant
houses.

It was noticed in eight selected blocks of four districts that no efforts were made by
the State/DRDAs/Blocks to facilitate the use of local, cost effective, disaster
resistance and environment friendly technologies so as to make economic and
efficient IAY houses. The beneficiaries were not given any expert advice while
construction/up gradation of the IAY houses.

The Deputy Director, C&RD stated (August 2013) that IAY being an old scheme,
officers dealing with the implementation of the scheme were acquainted with the
construction of the houses. There was no separate fund under the scheme earmarked
for training of the officers to facilitate the use of local, cost effective, disaster
resistance and environmental friendly technologies. The fact remains that the State
Government has not formalised the system even after 17 years of implementation of
IAY in the State.

1.2.9.5 Absence of uniform type design

Para 5.3 of the [AY guidelines provides that each State Government will finalise its
type designs for IAY houses along with technical and material specifications. It is not
necessary to have only one type design and one state can adopt more than one type
designs depending upon local conditions.

In two out of four selected districts viz. West Garo Hills and East Garo Hills, the
DRDAs had prescribed type design for the IAY houses. However, in another two
selected districts namely East Khasi Hills and West Khasi Hills, there were no type
designs for IAY houses and beneficiaries constructed their houses as per their need
and choice within the prescribed monetary limits.

The Deputy Director, C&RD stated (August 2013) that the State Government had not
prescribed any type design for IAY houses. However, it was left to the DRDAs for



Chapter I - Social Sector

preparation of type design and plan and estimate according to the local climatic
conditions.

1.2.9.6 Implementation of the Homestead scheme

As per Para 8.1 of the IAY guidelines, a scheme for providing homestead sites to
those rural BPL households who have neither agricultural land nor a housesite was
launched as a part of IAY with effect from 24 August 2009.

Further, as per the direction of the MoRD (October 2009), the States were “to provide
homestead sites to all by 2012” by regularizing the presently occupied land, if any, or
by allotting surplus Government land and for this purpose, district wise number of
homestead sites were to be identified for purchase/acquisition.

However, homestead scheme has not been implemented in the State till date.

In Tikrikilla block (out of eight selected blocks), Audit observed that a list of 34
landless rural BPL households was submitted by the BDO (July 2010) to the Project
Director, DRDA, West Garo Hills. However, no action in respect of identification of
site for purchase/acquisition of land was taken by the State Government (as of July
2013).

The Deputy Director, C&RD stated (August 2013) that in rural areas of the State, land
is always owned by the community and the community is always ready to part its land
for construction of IAY houses. The reply is not acceptable as the list of 34 landless
rural BPL households in Tikrikilla block prepared by the BDO indicated that there
were still landless rural BPL households in need of land for constructing [AY houses.
Further, National Level Monitors has also recommended for implementation of
jomestead scheme in the State.

1.2.9.7 Differential Rate of Interest scheme

Paras 3.1.1 and 3.1.2 of IAY guidelines provide that in addition to the assistance
rovided under the scheme, a beneficiary could avail a loan up to % 20,000 per
ousing unit under the Differential Rate of Interest (DRI) scheme at an interest rate of
4 per cent per annum DRDAs were to facilitate availing of such loan to the
eneficiary. Audit observed that no such credit facility was availed by any IAY
eneficiary in the State.

The Deputy Director, C&RD stated (August 2013) that the DRI scheme had been
widely publicised in the State through the BDOs and Village Headmen so that the
eneficiaries who were willing to avail the scheme could come up with application for
the same. It was further stated that the matter had been discussed with the banks at
district level and since no beneficiary had come forward to avail the scheme, it could
not be implemented. The assertion of Deputy Director, C&RD has to be viewed in
ackground of the fact that the beneficiary survey of 540 households conducted in
eight selected blocks of four selected districts revealed that they were not even aware
of the existence of such facilities.



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

1.2.9.8 Findings of Joint Inspection

Beneficiary Survey in the form of Joint Inspection comprising of the audit team,
Junior Engineers and Gram Sevaks of the selected cight blocks (except Songsak
block, East Garo Hills) covering 540 beneficiaries was conducted during May to July
2013. The findings of the Joint inspection are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs:

> Registration of IAY house in the name of female beneficiary

Para 2.4 of the IAY guidelines provides that allotment of dwelling units should be in
the name of female member of the beneficiary household. Alternatively, it can be
allotted in the name of both husband and wife. However, if there is no eligible female
member in the family available/alive, house can be allotted to the male member of a
deserving BPL family.

The position of allotment of IAY houses to the beneficiaries in the State is detailed

clow:

> Display of the IAY logo

Para 5.10 of the IAY guidelines provides that on completion of an IAY dwelling unit,
the DRDA concerned should ensure that for each house so constructed, a display
oard is fixed indicating the Gol Rural Housing logo, year of construction, name of

From the above, it was evident that the instruction regarding display of IAY logo was

the IAY logo while 20 beneficiaries were not even aware of it.

Hence, the fact remains that the DRDAs, who were responsible for ensuring display
of IAY logos, did not discharge their responsibility as envisaged in the guidelines.

> Involvement of Beneficiary in the construction of house under IAY

ara 2.3 of the IAY guidelines inter alia provides that involvement of beneficiaries in
construction of IAY houses results in greater satisfaction and acceptability of the
ouses by the beneficiaries. Beneficiary survey of 540 beneficiaries revealed that only
55 houses were constructed by engaging family members and in other cases
eneficiaries had to rely upon daily wage labourers.

1.2.10 Convergence with other flagship schemes

Objective 4: Whether the convergence of the IAY activities with other
programmes as envisaged was effectively achieved and ensured
availability of a complete functional dwelling unit.

Para 5.11 of the IAY guidelines provides that the DRDAs will make concerted efforts
to identify the programme/schemes being implemented by various
Ministries/Departments of the Gol like Total Sanitation Campaign (TSC) for
constructing sanitary latrines, Rajiv Gandhi Grameen Vidyutikaran Yojana (RGGVY)
for providing free electricity connections and National Rural Water Supply
Programme (NRWSP) for making provision of drinking water to IAY houses.

Audit observed that four selected DRDAs had not taken any initiative to coordinate
with other departments in identifying the schemes/programme that could be
converged with IAY. The following position further established during the joint
inspection carried out by Audit along with the Junior Engineers and Gram Sevaks of
the selected eight blocks:

Table 1.2.19

No. of houses having
Total No. of Sanitary Water | Electricity
beneficiaries | Kitchen | Smokeless | ventitation | feility | Drainage | supply supply
chulha under facility under under
TSC NRWSP RGGVY
540 236 47 340 108 51 14 122
From the above, it was evident that:

> Out of 540 beneficiaries covered during joint inspection, 236 beneficiaries
were having kitchen, of which only 47 had smokeless chulhas.

> 340 (63 per cent) out of 540 IAY houses were provided with ventilation
facilities.



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

> Sanitary facilities under TSC was only 108 (20 per cent) out of 540
beneficiaries were having sanitation facilities.

> Out of 540 IAY houses, only 122 (23 per cent) were having electricity
connections under RGGVY.

Vv

Water supply under NRWSP was negligible, 1.6 out of 540 IAY houses only
14 (3 per cent) were made drinking water available under this scheme.

Thus, basic amenities as provided in the guidelines were not provided in the houses
constructed under IAY. Consequently, the beneficiaries had been deprived of the
basic facilities of a clean environment, sanitation and infrastructure.

The Deputy Director, C&RD stated (August 2013) that the tradition of the people do
not favour installation of smokeless chulhas. The reply is silent about shortfall in
other amenities.

Thus, due to lack of coordination of DRDAs with other departments in identifying the
schemes/programme to be converged with IAY, the purpose of providing quality
houses with all intended benefits under various Government programmes could not be
achieved.

1.2.11 Monitoring and Evaluation

Objective 5: Whether the mechanism in place for monitoring and evaluation of
the outcomes of the programme was adequate and effective.

1.2.11.1 Inspection by State/District/Block level functionaries

Para 6.1 of IAY guidelines provides that officers dealing with the IAY at the State
headquarters should visit districts regularly and ascertain whether the programme was
being implemented satisfactorily and construction of houses was being carried out in
accordance with the prescribed procedure. Likewise, officers at the district and block
levels were to closely monitor all aspects of the IAY through visits to the worksites. A
Schedule of inspection which prescribes a minimum number of field visits for each
supervisory level functionary from the State level to the block level was to be drawn
up and strictly adhered to.

Audit observed that the C&RD Department did not prescribe any schedule of
inspection indicating minimum number of field visits for each supervisory level
officer at the State, district and block level. As such, inspections were carried out in
casual, haphazard and ad hoc manner.

The Deputy Director, C&RD stated (August 2013) that no schedule of inspection was
prepared at any level. However, inspections were carried out at different intervals.
The JEs at block level carried out the technical supervision as the release of fund is
based on the physical progress of the construction of the houses. It was also stated that
there was no separate fund under the scheme earmarked for monitoring through field
inspections and the fund released by the Gol was meant for construction of houses
only. Shortage of office vehicle at different levels also hindered the monitoring of

20



Chapter I - Social Sector

109४ through inspection. The fact remains that there were casual, haphazard and
ad hoc field visits by the officers which could not ensure proper implementation of
TAY as envisaged in the guidelines.

1.2.11.2 State Level Vigilance and Monitoring Committee

IAY is to be monitored at the State level by the State Level Vigilance and Monitoring
Committee (SLVMC) headed by the Minister, C&RD Department as the Chairperson
and the Commissioner-cum-Secretary, C&RD Department as the Member Secretary.
A representative or nominee of the Ministry of Rural Development, Gol should
invariably be invited to participate in the meetings of the committee. The Committee
was primarily responsible for effective implementation of the IAY by ensuring better
monitoring and supervision.

Scrutiny of records revealed that the SLVMC which was constituted in October 2006
met only five times during the period under review. During the five times when the
committee met, no representative from the Ministry of Rural Development, Gol had
participated. As per the proceedings of the meeting, only the financial and physical
targets and achievements aspects were discussed. There were no records to show that
the committee discussed the ways and means to improve implementation of the
programme, remedial measures, proposals for new time lines etc.

The Deputy Director, C&RD stated (August 2013) that due to frequent change of the
Chairman and his busy schedule, meetings of the SLVMC could be held only five
times during the period under review. Holding of these meetings was informed to the
MoRD but no representative could come to attend the meeting.

1.2.11.3 Social Audit

Paras 6.3.1 and 6.3.5 of the IAY guidelines provide that Social audit should be in
place in order to ensure greater transparency in the implementation of IAY at various
levels and hinges on the assumption that people should have access to information
about implementation of these programmes in all their aspects.

Audit observed that the system of social audit was neither in place nor any social
audit was conducted during the period covered by audit. As a result, audit could not
ascertain whether transparency in the implementation of [AY in the State has been
ensured.

While accepting the Audit observation, the Deputy Director, C&RD_ stated
(September 2013) that social audit could not be conducted because there was no clear
cut instruction or guidelines on the manner in which social audit should be conducted.
Besides, no separate fund was provided for conducting the same.

1.2.11.4 Central Monitoring and Evaluation: AwaasSoft

The Ministry launched (July 2010) a web-based MIS programme called AwaasSoft to
capture beneficiary-wise data to monitor IAY. AwaasSoft is a local language enabled
workflow based transaction level Management Information System to facilitate

21


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

e-governance in the System. This is a tool for management, generate all reports, funds
released, progress in construction of houses and tracks convergence of all benefits.
The MIS will be accessible not only to all the stake holders including beneficiaries but
also citizens at large.

Audit observed that though the AwaasSoft was introduced in the State in 2010, data
entry and uploading of data is very slow. The Deputy Director, C&RD stated
(September 2013) that slow progress in uploading of data was due to poor
connectivity and absence of manpower at the DRDA level. Such slow progress in
uploading data would have an adverse affect in release of Gol’s second instalment
which is linked with the expenditure generated through the AwaasSoft from the year
2013-14.

1.2.11.5 Implementation of recommendations made by National Level Monitors

A comprehensive system of National Level Monitoring (NLM) has been evolved by
the MoRD by involving public spirited senior level retired Civil/Defence Service
Officers and Academia, willing to provide voluntary services for a public cause. The
basic premise is that by involving third party independent monitors, unbiased and
objective monitoring can be achieved. The role of the NLMs in monitoring the
programmes of the Ministry has become more important in the recent years due to
significant increase in the allocation of funds for various rural development
programmes and also due to the increase in demands of accountability and
transparency in the execution of these programmes.

Reports of the NLM of three® out of four selected districts made available to audit
showed that the main recommendations of the NLMs were in the areas of display of
IAY logos, providing homestead sites to the landless rural BPL households, and
increasing the IAY assistance so as to build a minimum standard of house. However,
audit observed that the display of IAY logos and scheme of Homestead sites to the
landless BPL households have not been implemented in the State till date (August
2013). Moreover there were no records available which Audit could verify relating to
increase in IAY assistance.

1.2.11.6 Evaluation Study

Para 6.2 of the IAY guidelines provides that the Central Government may conduct
periodical evaluation studies on the implementation of IAY. These Evaluation studies
were to be conducted by institutions and organisations of repute on the
implementation as well as the issues thrown up by the Concurrent Evaluation,
conducted by the Gol. The States may also conduct Evaluation Studies on their own
regarding the implementation and impact of the programme in their State. Remedial
action should be taken by the States on the basis of the observations made in these
evaluation studies and also in the Concurrent Evaluation conducted by or on behalf of
Gol.

6 (East Khasi Hills-3, West Khasi Hills-2 and West Garo Hills-3)

22


Chapter I - Social Sector

Audit observed that to conduct an evaluation study on implementation of IAY for the
period 2007-08 to 2011-12 in the State, the State Government (Programme
Implementation and Evaluation Department) had entrusted (November 2011) an
Orissa based firm viz D.J Research & Consultancy Pvt. Ltd. with the task for which an
amount of % 4.30 lakh was paid (November 2011) by the Department. The firm in its
report (June 2012) recommended for enhancement of assistance under IAY,
rectification of the BPL list, transparency in selection of beneficiaries, convergence of
scheme and strong monitoring and supervision.

It was noticed that none of the recommendations of the Evaluation study had been
implemented till date (August 2013). Reason for not acting upon on any of the
recommendations was not available on record.

1.2.12 Conclusion

The objective of IAY in providing housing to the rural poor was not achieved as
envisaged due to lapses in planning, financial management, implementation and
monitoring of the scheme.

There were instances of extending benefit to ineligible beneficiaries at the cost of
eligible ones. Due to poor financial management central assistance were
curtailed. There were instances of undue delays in release of state share.

Implementation of [AY was not satisfactory as there were shortfalls in
achievement of targets during the period under review. Inventory of [AY houses
were not maintained. No efforts were made by the DRDAs/Blocks to facilitate the
use of local materials, cost effective and disaster resistant materials in the
construction of IAY houses.

In the absence of coordination with other departments, convergence of IAY with
other flagship schemes like TSC, NRWSP, RGGVY, MGNREGA, efc. was
inadequate and ineffective in the State.

Monitoring mechanism of the scheme was inadequate and ineffective. Absence of
schedule of inspection and lack of adequate field visits by supervisory officers
resulted in poor implementation of the scheme.

1.2.13 Recommendations

> The State Government should scrupulously take necessary steps for
implementation of the various aspects as envisaged in the revised
guidelines issued by Ministry of Rural Development, Gol in June 2013 for
more effective implementation of the Scheme.

> The State Government should develop the implementation strategy for
IAY by balancing the needs and advantages of habitation/cluster
approach and individual household approach. Socio-Economic Caste
Census (SECC) 2011 data should be used to identify the landless

23


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

households and also to revise the existing priority list for IAY. The State
Government should prioritise the habitations on the basis of transparent
criteria with a five year and annual priority list.

The State Government should conduct a detailed survey with the
involvement of the Panchayats to collect details of houses as well as other
facilities in the habitations like physical connectivity, power connectivity,
water supply, environmental sanitation and social infrastructure and list
out all eligible beneficiaries from the selected habitation.

State Government should ensure timely utilisation of available funds as
well as timely release of its matching share.

The State Government should take initiative to identify the landless rural
BPL households and provide homestead sites for construction of IAY
houses.

Completion of all the [AY houses within prescribed time limit should be
ensured. The DRDAs and BDOs should maintain proper inventory of
JAY houses in the prescribed format.

The State Government should make efforts to converge the
programmes/schemes being implemented by various Ministries/
Departments and put in place an appropriate system which facilitates
(i) construction of toilets, (ii) provision of portable drinking water and
(iii) provide electrification to all [AY households

Schedule of monitoring and supervision at each level should be
established to ensure accountability of implementing agencies.
Recommendations made by the NLMs and Evaluation Study Teams
should strictly be adhered to.

The matter was reported to Government in November 2013; reply had not been
received (February 2014).

Chapter I - Social Sector

HEALTH & FAMILY WELFARE DEPARTMENT

1.3. Unfruitful expenditure on establishment of Drug Testing
Laboratory

The Department failed to make a Drug Testing Laboratory functional even after
eight years thereby rendering an expenditure of ¢ 80.39 lakh unfruitful.

Under the Centrally Sponsored Scheme for Quality Control of Ayurveda, Siddha,
Unnani and Homoeopathy drugs, the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare,
Government of India (Gol) approved (January 2003) ‘Strengthening and setting up of
State Drug Testing Laboratory (DTL)’ with the objective to ensure quality of drugs
under Indian System of Medicine and Homoeopathy(ISM&H). The Gol would
provide up to % 1 crore to set up a new DTL, which was to be run with the State’s
own resources and with three Scientific Officers and four supporting staff.

On the basis of a proposal of the Government of Meghalaya (GoM), Gol sanctioned
(January 2004) हें 88.62 lakh (Building: हैं 25 lakh; Equipment, furniture, reference
books and computer stationery: हैं 57.66 lakh; Manpower: हैं 5.96 lakh) to be utilised
within 12 months for setting up one DTL for ISM&H drugs at Pasteur Institute,
Shillong. The Department took two years time for getting the sanction of the State
Finance Department, inviting and finalisation of tenders and issue of work order.
Finally, the work order for construction of building for the DTL was issued only in
January 2006. The work was completed in December 2006 at a cost of € 25 lakh and
the facility was taken over by the Directorate of Health Services (DHS), Research in
June 2009. The delay of two and half years in taking over the facility was attributed
(December 2013) by the Executive Engineer (HEW), DHS to delay in making
payment and for execution of some minor works like wiring, painting, etc. The DHS
incurred an expenditure of = 49.60 lakh on procurement of equipment and chemicals,
etc. and हैं 5.79 lakh on payment of salaries to two Scientific Officers appointed in
June 2010 on contract basis. Besides, one Ayurvedic physician was posted to the DTL
in September 2011.

It was further noticed that a proposal to create two posts of Scientific Officers and six
supporting staff required for effective functioning of the DTL was sent by the DHS to
GoM only in September 2011. Of this, two posts of Scientific Officers and five
supporting staff were sanctioned by GoM only in October 2013. However, these posts
were yet to be filled up.

Thus, there were delays at every stage right from completion of the DTL building,
taking over of the completed building, procurement of equipment and chemicals and
submitting of proposal for the required staff. Even the manpower required for
functioning of the laboratory, though sanctioned belatedly, were not engaged.
Consequently, the DTL could not be made fully operational even after eight years of
the stipulated date (January 2005) rendering the expenditure of % 80.39 lakh

25



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

unfruitful, besides frustrating the objective of ensuring quality control of drugs in the
State.

Government stated (September 2013) that most of the equipment required for the
laboratory had been procured, but did not furnish any reason for delay of over eight
years in making the laboratory fully operational.

1.4 Failure to enforce bond conditions

The Health & Family Welfare Department did not have a system in place to
ensure that students selected by it for MBBS course complied with the conditions
of the bond executed by them. As a result, the shortage of doctors in the
Department had increased over the years. Further, bond money of % 6.70 crore
was not realised from 67 students.

Government of India (Gol), Ministry of Health & Family Welfare allocates quotas of
Bachelor of Medicine & Bachelor of Surgery (MBBS) course to States/Union
Territories who do not have medical colleges of their own to enable students from
these states to pursue studies in this discipline. Meghalaya is one such recipient from
this central pool. Against medical seats allotted to Meghalaya, Health & Family
Welfare (H&FW) Department, Government of Meghalaya (GoM) sponsors the
students who are selected on merit basis and as per the State’s reservation policy. On
selection, the students are required to execute a ‘bond cum agreement’ (bond) which
inter alia stipulates that the student:

> diligently prosecute and complete the said course at the said college;

> upon completing the MBBS course inform the H&FW Department of this fact
within 30 days;

> shall expressly request permission from the H&FW Department if he/she
desires to pursue post-graduate studies and if such permission is granted,
he/she shall within 30 days from the date of completing the post-graduate
course intimate this fact to the H&FW Department; and,

> on completion of medical studies, he/she shall join the Meghalaya Health
Service and serve in a rural area, posted by Government for a minimum period
of five years at such remuneration as may be prescribed.

In the event of breach of any of the above conditions, the student and his/her
father/mother/legal guardian shall jointly and severally become liable to pay the
amount laid down in the bond (fixed at हैं 10 lakh for the academic years 2000-10 and
thereafter revised to हैं 25 lakh). The duration of study including internship for a
MBBS course is 5 % years.

During 2003-08, the Department sponsored 215 students for MBBS course. All these
215 students should have completed their studies between 2008-09 and 2013-14 (up
to October 2013). Directorate of H&FW Department furnished information (July and

26



Chapter I - Social Sector

November 2013) in respect of only 194 students it has sponsored and no information
regarding remaining 21 students was furnished. As per information furnished by the
Department,

e 172 students have completed their MBBS course;
e While three students each have either discontinued their course or expired; and
e Remaining 16 were yet to complete their course.

Of the 172 students who have completed the MBBS course and should have joined
the Meghalaya Health Services and served in a rural area for a minimum period of
five years,

e only 95 have joined the Meghalaya Health Services and 5 more were awaiting
appointment under the Meghalaya Health Services;

¢ four students are pursuing post-graduate studies;

¢ one student who did not join the Meghalaya Health Services and in lieu paid
the bond amount; and

e remaining 67 students who have completed the MBBS course have not joined
the Meghalaya Health Services as per conditions of the bond-cum-agreement
executed by him/her.

However, the scrutiny of the records revealed that the Department had not invoked the
bond conditions and initiated necessary action to recover the amount of % 6.70 crore’
due from 67 students who have not joined the Meghalaya Health Services. It is
apparent that the H&FW Department did not have a monitoring mechanism in place
to collect regular information about students selected by it who have completed their
MBBS courses and to invoke the bond conditions for students who did not join
Meghalaya Health Service.

Audit noted that the vacancies of doctors had increased from 5 per cent (15 doctors)
as on 31 March 2009 of the sanctioned strength of doctors in the H&FW Department
to 21 per cent (92 doctors) as on 31 March 2013. This situation could have been
averted if the H&FW Department had a system in place to monitor and ensure
compliance of the bond conditions by the students selected for MBBS courses.

The matter was reported to Government in December 2013; reply had not been
received (February 2014).

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

1.5 Undue financial benefit to a pharmaceutical firm manufacturing
drugs without fulfilment of statutory requirements

The Health and Family Welfare Department extended undue patronage to an
unlicensed pharmaceutical firm which was also functioning without compliance
of ‘Good Manufacturing Practices’ and extended undue financial benefit of
र₹ 2.50 crore on purchase of drugs from this firm for use in Government hospitals
and health centres.

The Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940 and Rules framed thereunder stipulates that the
usiness of import, manufacture, distribution and sale of drugs in India can only be
carried out under licence. In Meghalaya, the Assistant Controller of Drugs (ACD),
Government of Meghalaya (GoM) is the licensing authority for the purpose of
enforcement of the Act and Rules which inter alia includes issuing licences to private
arties in the State to manufacture, distribute and sell drugs. In December 2001,
Government of India (Gol) amended the Rules to prescribe ‘Good Manufacturing
ractices’ (GMP) relating to premises, plants, equipment needed to be evolved by the
manufacturers of drugs and all drug manufacturers were obligated to be GMP
compliant from January 2004 (later extended from 1“ July 2005).

M/s Induscreed (firm), a small scale pharmaceutical unit in the State was granted a
icence in April 1998 to manufacture, distribute and sell certain types of oral liquids,
tablets and capsules*. The licence was renewed in June 2002 for a period of five years
from January 2002 to December 2006.

Test check (August 2013) of records of Director of Health Services, Medical
Institutions (DHS-MI), Meghalaya revealed that since the firm was not ‘Good
Manufacturing Practices’ (GMP) compliant, the ACD directed (July 2004 and
September 2004) the firm to rectify the deficiencies. In June 2006 a committee
comprising the Assistant Drugs Controller’, two Senior Inspectors of Drugs and one
Inspector of Drugs again inspected the firm’s premises and recommended that the
firm should stop production forthwith and construct a GMP compliant factory. The
DHS-MI, accordingly directed (September 2006) the firm to stop production
forthwith and to construct a GMP compliant factory. The DHS-MI also informed
(February 2007) the Health and Family Welfare (H&FW) Department, GoM that the
order to stop production was issued to the firm in pursuance to the direction/policy
adopted by Gol and also clarified that show cause notice was not at all necessary in
this case, as the firm was given adequate opportunity to comply with the prescribed
statutory requirements. But the H&FW Department directed (March 2007) the DHS-
MI to revoke his order of September 2006 against the firm. In response, the DHS-MI
proposed (March 2007) that the order might be revoked subject to compliance of the
defects pointed out in the inspection report of June 2006 within six months.

8 The approval granted to the firm to manufacture tablets and capsules was withdrawn in July 2004
° Assistant Drugs Controller, Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation, GOI, Kolkata.

28



Chapter I - Social Sector

The H&FW Department, GoM instead of ensuring that the firm complies with the
provisions of the Act and ignoring the proposal (March 2007) of the DHS-MI,
directed (April 2007) the DHS-MI to revoke its order of September 2006 as procedure
prescribed as per the provisions of the Act for ordering the closure of production, such
as issue of formal show cause notice prior to issuing the order of stopping the
production was not complied with. Accordingly, the DHS-MI on 16 April 2007
revoked its order of September 2006.

Scrutiny of records, further revealed that the firm did not apply for renewal of licence
after its expiry in December 2006. The firm without having a valid licence, however,
continued to manufacture drugs till October 2012 after which it ceased production on
its own due to renovation/construction of its factory.

Despite the firm not being GMP compliant or having a valid licence, the
Departmental Purchase Board (DPB) of the H&FW Department in its meetings (July
& October 2007) wrongly certified that the firm was provisionally given a certificate
of GMP and accordingly accepted the tender of the firm. Between June 2008 and
October 2012, the DHS-MI procured drugs worth %2.50 crore for use in GoM
hospitals and health centres from an unlicenced and non GMP compliant firm. Of this,
17 types of drugs worth % 1.26 crore purchased from the firm were not approved by
the DPB. Further, out of the drugs purchased, one drug (Tolu Syrup) was subsequently
tested (May 2010) by the Regional Drugs Testing Laboratory, Guwahati as ‘not of
standard quality’ and another drug (Albendazole Suspension) was reported by the
Ganesh Das Hospital, Shillong as ‘below the standard set by Indian Pharmacopoeia’.

Thus, instead of ensuring compliance with the provisions of Act and Rules, the
H&FW Department directed the DHS-MI to allow an unlicensed and non-GMP
compliant pharmaceutical firm to continue manufacturing drugs. The H&FW
Department/DHS-MI by wrongly certifying the firm as GMP-compliant, irregularly
procured drugs worth % 2.50 crore from it for use in GoM hospitals and health centres
resulting in extension of undue financial benefit. Such action of the H&FW
Department/DHS was not only injudicious but also fraught with risk of affecting the
health of the patients visiting the government hospitals and health centres.

The matter was reported to the Government in October 2013; reply had not been
received (February 2014).

2.1.1 Planning and conduct of Audit

Audit process starts with the assessment of risks faced by various departments of
Government based on expenditure incurred, criticality/complexity of activities, level
of delegated financial powers, assessment of overall internal controls and concerns.
The audits were conducted during 2012-13 involving expenditure of = 141.49 crore
(including expenditure pertaining to previous years audited during the year) of the
State Government under General Sector. The chapter contains two Compliance Audit
Paragraphs.


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

After completion of audit of each unit, Inspection Reports containing audit findings
are issued to the heads of the departments. The departments are requested to furnish
replies to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the Inspection Reports.
Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either settled or further action for
compliance is advised. The important audit observations arising out of these
Inspection Reports are processed for inclusion in the Audit Reports, which are
submitted to the Governor of State under Article 151 of the Constitution of India.

The major observations under General Sector detected in audit during the year
2012-13 are given below.

Chapter II — General Sector

HOME (PRISONS) DEPARTMENT

2.2... Unfruitful expenditure on construction of two jails

Arbitrary action of the Public Works Department in execution of works for
Nongstoin and Nongpoh jails contrary to the State’s Financial Rules and
exorbitant delay at every stage resulted in an unfruitful expenditure of % 8.40
crore.

The Shillong jail which in addition to East Khasi Hills District, was also catering to
the two neighbouring districts (West Khasi Hills and Ri Bhoi). To overcome the
problem of overcrowding of Shillong jail, Government of Meghalaya (GoM) acquired
land in August 1997 and October 1999 to set up two new jails at Nongstoin, West
Khasi Hills and Nongpoh, Ri-Bhoi respectively at a total cost of = 78.28 lakh'. Under
the scheme “Modernisation of Prisons” Ministry of Home Affairs, Government of
India (Gol), sanctioned in March 2003 and December 2003, construction of two
jails at Nongstoin and Nongpoh respectively. This scheme was launched by Gol in
2002-03 and was operative till 2006-07 (later extended up to 2008-09) and any
additional fund required on account of revision of estimate to complete the project
beyond the extended period of the scheme was to be borne by the State Government.
GoM accorded Administrative Approval” (AA) for construction of both the jails at an
estimated cost of % 12.55 crore’. The State Public Works Department (PWD) was to
construct both the jails by 2006-07, for which हैं 12.12 crore was released to the PWD.
While conveying the administrative approval for the jail at Nongpoh, the Assistant
Chief Engineer (Buildings) (ACE) directed (March 2004) the Executive Engineer
(EE), PWD (Buildings), Shillong to submit detailed estimate for technical sanction
before actual execution of work and in case of cost escalation, to submit the revised
estimate immediately.

Test-check (June and October 2012) of records of the Director General of Prisons and
Executive Engineer, PWD Building Division, Shillong revealed that both the jails
have not been completed till date (March 2013). The works in Nongstoin and
Nongpoh jail have been stalled since December 2009 and March 2010 after a physical
progress of 72 per cent and 85 per cent at an expenditure of % 2.80 crore and हैं 4.82
crore respectively. The balance amount of ¥ 4.50 crore (out of € 12.12 crore released
to the PWD) is lying under ‘8443-PW Deposit-III’.

Further scrutiny revealed that in contravention of the mandatory requirement
stipulated in Rule 243 of the Meghalaya Financial Rules, 1981 (MFR) that for every
work (except petty works) a detailed estimate must be prepared for technical sanction
of the competent authority which must be obtained before commencement of
construction and ignoring the instructions of the ACE, construction works in both the

1 Nongstoin: 11 lakh; Nongpoh: & 67.29 lakh
2 Administrative Approval: Nongstoin jail on 16 August, 2004 and Nongpoh jail on 11 March, 2004
- Nongstoin: हैं 6.26 crore; Nongpoh: हैं 6.29 crore.

33



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

jails were started before preparation of the detailed estimate and obtaining the same
technically sanctioned. In addition, Audit also observed that there were delays at
every stage, which are discussed below:-

(७). Nongstoin Jail

The work was split into 20 components. Tenders for different components were
invited in three instalments from July 2004 to as late as November 2006 (i.e. after a
lapse of 27 months from the issue of AA). The final work orders for only 16
components of work were issued between November 2004 and August 2007 after
considerable delays. Final work orders for the remaining four components of work
(all related to construction of Security wall/Boundary fencing) were yet to be issued
(as of March 2013) due to non-payment of Security Deposit or failure to sign the
contract agreement by the selected contractors. However, no action was taken to allot
the works to the next eligible contractors. As per final work orders these components
of works were scheduled for completion between November 2005 and August 2008.
Even after the issue of final work order, some contractors could not commence the
work due to non-availability of site/building layout in time by the Division.
Meanwhile, the detailed revised estimate was submitted only in January 2006 after a
delay of two years from the issue of AA. The total estimated cost of the work
increased to 7.98 crore against the preliminary estimate of € 6.26 crore.

The construction works remained suspended since December 2009 due to
non-sanctioning of the detailed/revised estimate. Till then, physical progress of
72 per cent was achieved after incurring an expenditure of % 2.80 crore.

Thereafter, the estimates were revised twice, first in February 2010 to हैं 9.81 crore
and the second in July 2012 to हैं 16.87 crore.

(B) Nongpoh jail

The works for construction of Nongpoh jail was split into 21 components. Tenders for
different components were invited in three instalments from June 2004 to November
2006 (i.e. after a lapse of 3 to 32 months from the issue of AA). The final work orders
for all the 21 components were issued between November 2004 and January 2010
after considerable delays. As per final work orders, these components of works were
scheduled for completion between March 2006 and June 2010. However, like in the
case of Nongstoin jail, even after the issue of final work order, some contractors could
not commence the work due to non-availability of site/building layout in time by the
Division. The construction works remained suspended since March 2010 due to
non-sanctioning of the detailed/revised estimate. Till then, physical progress of
85 per cent was achieved after incurring an expenditure of % 4.82 crore. Meanwhile,
three detailed/revised estimates have been submitted so far; first in November 2007
for = 8.84 crore; second in September 2009 for हैं 9.06 crore and third in September
2012 for = 10.77 crore.

In both the cases, none of the detailed/revised estimates had been sanctioned by the
GoM till date (March 2013).

34


Chapter II — General Sector

The Director General of Prisons, Meghalaya stated (June 2013) that due to price
escalation, the amount released by Government was not enough for completion of the
jails. The EE, Shillong Building Division stated (June 2013) that the delay in
completion of the jails was due to non-sanction of revised estimate by GoM. The
replies are not acceptable because timely action was not initiated by the executing
department to ascertain the actual cost of the jails through preparation of a detailed
estimate, which led to further escalation in the cost of the works.

Thus, due to arbitrary action of the PWD to commence works of both Nongstoin and
Nongpoh jails without a technically sanctioned detailed estimate in contravention of
the MFR and undue delay at every stage resulted in cost escalation. Consequently,
these jails remained incomplete even after six years of the scheduled target of
2006-07 rendering the expenditure of % 8.40 crore’ unfruitful, besides frustrating the
intended objectives. Since the scheme under which the construction of both the jails
sanctioned by Gol had been closed in 2008-09, the possibility of completion of these
jails in near future is remote, due to resource crunch in the State as the additional cost
of € 15.52 crore’ has to be borne by the State Government for completion.

The matter was reported to Government in September 2013; reply had not been
received (February 2014).

HOME (POLICE) DEPART T

2.3 Idle expenditure on procurement of boots in excess of requirement

Twelve police formations had boots worth हैं 76.71 lakh in stock in excess of
requirement as at the end of March 2013.

The Meghalaya Police Handbook, 1991 lays down that personnel of the rank of
constable to havildar/assistant sub-inspector are to be issued a pair of ‘ammunition
boots’ (hereinafter referred to as ‘boots’) annually. The Assistant Inspector General of
Police (Administration) [AIG] every year places the supply order for boots for the
State’s police force.

The prescribed average lifespan of the boots is one year. As such, the entire process of
requirement, tendering, purchase and issue to the formations is a cyclical annual event
which needs to commence and conclude within a period of 12 months so that all
eligible recipients receive fresh pair after lapse of 12 months from the date of previous
issue.

+ ₹ 7.62 crore being the expenditure incurred on the two jails up to March 2010 plus % 0.78 crore being
the cost of acquiring the land for both the jails.

> Latest revised estimated cost: हें 27.64 crore - Expenditure so far incurred: % 7.62 crore = % 20.02 crore -
₹ 4.50 crore = & 15.52 crore.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

3.1.1 Planning and conduct of Audit

Audit process starts with the assessment of risks faced by various departments of the
Government based on expenditure incurred, criticality/complexity of activities, level
of delegated financial powers, assessment of overall internal controls and concerns.
The audits were conducted during 2012-13 involving expenditure of % 759.45 crore
(including expenditure pertaining to previous years audited during the year) of the
State Government under Economic Sector. The chapter contains two performance
audits on ‘Roads & Bridges funded by Non Lapsable Central Pool of Resources and
North Eastern Council’ and ‘Tourism Development Activities’ and three Compliance
Audit paragraphs.

After completion of audit of each unit, Inspection Reports containing audit findings
are issued to the heads of the departments. The departments are requested to furnish
replies to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the Inspection Reports.
Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either settled or further action for
compliance is advised. The important audit observations arising out of these
Inspection Reports are processed for inclusion in the Audit Reports, which are
submitted to the Governor of State under Article 151 of the Constitution of India.

The major observations under Economic Sector detected in audit during the year
2012-13 are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

38


Chapter III - Economic Sector

PUBLIC WORKS DEPART

3.2 Roads & Bridges funded by Non Lapsable Central Pool of Resources
and North Eastern Council

Highlights

One of the objectives of the Non-Lapsable Central Pool of Resources (NLCPR) and
the North Eastern Council (NEC) was to develop infrastructure, especially
construction of Roads and Bridges (R&B). Though the assets created by the NLCPR
and the NEC funds have helped the State to bridge the infrastructural gap to some
extent, certain aspects like poor planning, ineffective financial management, tardy
implementation and lack of proper monitoring and supervision adversely affected
the implementation of these projects.

The State Planning Department neither submitted the priority lists in time nor
was proper analysis of infrastructural gaps conducted.

(Paragraph 3.2.7.1)

Huge amount of funds lying unutilised with State Finance Department indicates
that the PWD did not have effective arrangement to absorb the allotted funds
under NLCPR and NEC funded R&B projects.

(Paragraph 3.2.8)

PWD incurred an extra expenditure amounting to %2.08 crore towards
inadmissible items. Certain items of work were executed in excess of the
sanctioned provision which resulted in excess expenditure by an amount of & 5.89
crore.

(Paragraphs 3.2.9.5 & 3.2.9.8)

Allocation of same works to more than one contractor led to an avoidable
expenditure to the tune of % 1.59 crore. Injudicious decision of the PWD in
selecting the stone quarry led to an avoidable expenditure of रैं 97.90 lakh on
haulage.

(Paragraphs 3.2.9.7 & 3.2.10.1)
Funds amounting to हैं 1.38 crore were diverted from NLCPR and NEC funded

R&B projects to other works which fell under Special Plan Assistance and other
NEC projects.

(Paragraph 3.2.9.6)

39



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

3.2.1 Introduction

The Government of India created a Non-Lapsable Central Pool of Resources (NLCPR)
in the Union Budget for the year 1998-99 with a broad objective to ensure speedy
development of infrastructure in North Eastern Region (NER) of which Roads and
Bridges is of utmost importance specially in case of Meghalaya since the only mode of
transportation in the State is roads and is, therefore, the catalyst of all economic and
social activities.

The North Eastern Council (NEC) which came into existence in 1972 with the
objective to develop infrastructure, especially construction of Roads and Bridges
(R&B) in NER, is the regional planning body responsible for scrutinising the R&B
projects proposed by the State Government for obtaining approval.

This Performance Audit covers the NLCPR and the NEC funded R&B projects during
2008-09 to 2012-13 in Meghalaya.

Prior to 2008, a total road length of 1090.568 km had been sanctioned by NLCPR &
NEC in Meghalaya which were mostly upgradation/ improvement of existing road. Of
this, 672.165 km had been covered up to 2008. Thereafter, during 2008-13, 346 km
road length had been sanctioned while the total road length constructed during this
period was 260.448 km. Hence, the total incomplete road length at the end of March
2013 was 503.955 km.

3.2.2. Operational Set up

The State Planning Department is the Nodal Department for implementation of
NLCPR & NEC funded projects and all R&B projects were executed by State Public
Works Department (PWD). The operational set-up for implementation of NLCPR and
NEC funded R&B projects is shown below:

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013 (Social, General and Economic Sectors and PSUs)

3.2.3. Scope of Audit

Performance review of NLCPR and NEC funded R&B covering the period 2008-09 to
2012-13 was conducted (April-July 2013) through test-check of the records of the CE,
PWD (R) and Executive Engineers of 12 divisions’. Out of 31 NLCPR and 16 NEC
funded R&B projects (Appendix 3.2.1) under execution during 2008-13, 10 NLCPR
and five NEC funded projects (Appendix 3.2.2) were selected for detailed check on
the basis of probability proportionate to size without replacement method.

3.2.4 Audit Objectives

The objectives of the Performance Audit of the NLCPR and the NEC funded R&B
projects were to assess whether:

> the projects were planned appropriately and adequate infrastructural gap
analysis was done;

> adequate funds were released in a timely manner and utilised for the given
purpose;

> projects were executed efficiently and economically to achieve intended
objectives;

> adequate and effective monitoring and evaluation was done; and

> the department took action on the recommendations made by the Public

Accounts Committee.
3.2.5 Audit Criteria
The findings were benchmarked against the criteria drawn from the following sources:

> NLCPR Guidelines;

> Detailed Project Reports (DPRs);

> Conditions and norms of release of fund;

> Performance indicators relevant to the NLCPR & the NEC funded R&B
projects; and

> Prescribed monitoring mechanism in place.

3.2.6 Audit Methodology

Audit started with an entry conference which was held in May 2013, wherein the audit
objectives, criteria and methodology were discussed with the Secretary, PWD, Chief
Engineer, Roads [CE (R)], CE, National Highways, officials of Finance and Planning
Departments, Government of Meghalaya (GoM) and an official of the NEC. Further,
before arriving at audit conclusions, various items like adequacy of funds, utilisation
of funds, adherence to project guidelines, execution of various projects, efc. were
analysed.

' Projects were selected using Probability Proportional to Size without Replacement (PPSWOR)
method.

42


Chapter III - Economic Sector

Audit findings were discussed with officials of the PWD as well as Finance and
Planning Departments and NEC in an exit conference (280 November 2013) and the
replies of the Government/Department have been incorporated in the report at
appropriate places.
Acknowledgement

Audit acknowledges the co-operation extended by the officials of the PWD to Audit
personnel in carrying out this assignment.
